## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {

    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];

    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

# Brainstorming Ideas Into Designs

Help turn ideas into fully formed designs and specs through natural collaborative dialogue.

Start by understanding the current project context, then ask questions one at a time to refine the idea. Once you understand what you're building, present the design and get user approval.

<HARD-GATE>
Do NOT invoke any implementation skill, write any code, scaffold any project, or take any implementation action until you have presented a design and the user has approved it. This applies to EVERY project regardless of perceived simplicity.
</HARD-GATE>

## Anti-Pattern: "This Is Too Simple To Need A Design"

Every project goes through this process. A todo list, a single-function utility, a config change â€” all of them. "Simple" projects are where unexamined assumptions cause the most wasted work. The design can be short (a few sentences for truly simple projects), but you MUST present it and get approval.

## Checklist

You MUST create a task for each of these items and complete them in order:

1. **Explore project context** â€” check files, docs, recent commits
2. **Offer visual companion** (if topic will involve visual questions) â€” this is its own message, not combined with a clarifying question. See the Visual Companion section below.
3. **Ask clarifying questions** â€” one at a time, understand purpose/constraints/success criteria
4. **Propose 2-3 approaches** â€” with trade-offs and your recommendation
5. **Present design** â€” in sections scaled to their complexity, get user approval after each section
6. **Write design doc** â€” save to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md` and commit
7. **Spec self-review** â€” quick inline check for placeholders, contradictions, ambiguity, scope (see below)
8. **User reviews written spec** â€” ask user to review the spec file before proceeding
9. **Transition to implementation** â€” invoke writing-plans skill to create implementation plan

## Process Flow

```dot
digraph brainstorming {
    "Explore project context" [shape=box];
    "Visual questions ahead?" [shape=diamond];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [shape=box];
    "Ask clarifying questions" [shape=box];
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" [shape=box];
    "Present design sections" [shape=box];
    "User approves design?" [shape=diamond];
    "Write design doc" [shape=box];
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" [shape=box];
    "User reviews spec?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke writing-plans skill" [shape=doublecircle];

    "Explore project context" -> "Visual questions ahead?";
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" [label="yes"];
    "Visual questions ahead?" -> "Ask clarifying questions" [label="no"];
    "Offer Visual Companion\n(own message, no other content)" -> "Ask clarifying questions";
    "Ask clarifying questions" -> "Propose 2-3 approaches";
    "Propose 2-3 approaches" -> "Present design sections";
    "Present design sections" -> "User approves design?";
    "User approves design?" -> "Present design sections" [label="no, revise"];
    "User approves design?" -> "Write design doc" [label="yes"];
    "Write design doc" -> "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)";
    "Spec self-review\n(fix inline)" -> "User reviews spec?";
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Write design doc" [label="changes requested"];
    "User reviews spec?" -> "Invoke writing-plans skill" [label="approved"];
}
```

**The terminal state is invoking writing-plans.** Do NOT invoke frontend-design, mcp-builder, or any other implementation skill. The ONLY skill you invoke after brainstorming is writing-plans.

## The Process

**Understanding the idea:**

- Check out the current project state first (files, docs, recent commits)
- Before asking detailed questions, assess scope: if the request describes multiple independent subsystems (e.g., "build a platform with chat, file storage, billing, and analytics"), flag this immediately. Don't spend questions refining details of a project that needs to be decomposed first.
- If the project is too large for a single spec, help the user decompose into sub-projects: what are the independent pieces, how do they relate, what order should they be built? Then brainstorm the first sub-project through the normal design flow. Each sub-project gets its own spec â†’ plan â†’ implementation cycle.
- For appropriately-scoped projects, ask questions one at a time to refine the idea
- Prefer multiple choice questions when possible, but open-ended is fine too
- Only one question per message - if a topic needs more exploration, break it into multiple questions
- Focus on understanding: purpose, constraints, success criteria

**Exploring approaches:**

- Propose 2-3 different approaches with trade-offs
- Present options conversationally with your recommendation and reasoning
- Lead with your recommended option and explain why

**Presenting the design:**

- Once you believe you understand what you're building, present the design
- Scale each section to its complexity: a few sentences if straightforward, up to 200-300 words if nuanced
- Ask after each section whether it looks right so far
- Cover: architecture, components, data flow, error handling, testing
- Be ready to go back and clarify if something doesn't make sense

**Design for isolation and clarity:**

- Break the system into smaller units that each have one clear purpose, communicate through well-defined interfaces, and can be understood and tested independently
- For each unit, you should be able to answer: what does it do, how do you use it, and what does it depend on?
- Can someone understand what a unit does without reading its internals? Can you change the internals without breaking consumers? If not, the boundaries need work.
- Smaller, well-bounded units are also easier for you to work with - you reason better about code you can hold in context at once, and your edits are more reliable when files are focused. When a file grows large, that's often a signal that it's doing too much.

**Working in existing codebases:**

- Explore the current structure before proposing changes. Follow existing patterns.
- Where existing code has problems that affect the work (e.g., a file that's grown too large, unclear boundaries, tangled responsibilities), include targeted improvements as part of the design - the way a good developer improves code they're working in.
- Don't propose unrelated refactoring. Stay focused on what serves the current goal.

## After the Design

**Documentation:**

- Write the validated design (spec) to `docs/superpowers/specs/YYYY-MM-DD-<topic>-design.md`
  - (User preferences for spec location override this default)
- Use elements-of-style:writing-clearly-and-concisely skill if available
- Commit the design document to git

**Spec Self-Review:**
After writing the spec document, look at it with fresh eyes:

1. **Placeholder scan:** Any "TBD", "TODO", incomplete sections, or vague requirements? Fix them.
2. **Internal consistency:** Do any sections contradict each other? Does the architecture match the feature descriptions?
3. **Scope check:** Is this focused enough for a single implementation plan, or does it need decomposition?
4. **Ambiguity check:** Could any requirement be interpreted two different ways? If so, pick one and make it explicit.

Fix any issues inline. No need to re-review â€” just fix and move on.

**User Review Gate:**
After the spec review loop passes, ask the user to review the written spec before proceeding:

> "Spec written and committed to `<path>`. Please review it and let me know if you want to make any changes before we start writing out the implementation plan."

Wait for the user's response. If they request changes, make them and re-run the spec review loop. Only proceed once the user approves.

**Implementation:**

- Invoke the writing-plans skill to create a detailed implementation plan
- Do NOT invoke any other skill. writing-plans is the next step.

## Key Principles

- **One question at a time** - Don't overwhelm with multiple questions
- **Multiple choice preferred** - Easier to answer than open-ended when possible
- **YAGNI ruthlessly** - Remove unnecessary features from all designs
- **Explore alternatives** - Always propose 2-3 approaches before settling
- **Incremental validation** - Present design, get approval before moving on
- **Be flexible** - Go back and clarify when something doesn't make sense

## Visual Companion

A browser-based companion for showing mockups, diagrams, and visual options during brainstorming. Available as a tool â€” not a mode. Accepting the companion means it's available for questions that benefit from visual treatment; it does NOT mean every question goes through the browser.

**Offering the companion:** When you anticipate that upcoming questions will involve visual content (mockups, layouts, diagrams), offer it once for consent:
> "Some of what we're working on might be easier to explain if I can show it to you in a web browser. I can put together mockups, diagrams, comparisons, and other visuals as we go. This feature is still new and can be token-intensive. Want to try it? (Requires opening a local URL)"

**This offer MUST be its own message.** Do not combine it with clarifying questions, context summaries, or any other content. The message should contain ONLY the offer above and nothing else. Wait for the user's response before continuing. If they decline, proceed with text-only brainstorming.

**Per-question decision:** Even after the user accepts, decide FOR EACH QUESTION whether to use the browser or the terminal. The test: **would the user understand this better by seeing it than reading it?**

- **Use the browser** for content that IS visual â€” mockups, wireframes, layout comparisons, architecture diagrams, side-by-side visual designs
- **Use the terminal** for content that is text â€” requirements questions, conceptual choices, tradeoff lists, A/B/C/D text options, scope decisions

A question about a UI topic is not automatically a visual question. "What does personality mean in this context?" is a conceptual question â€” use the terminal. "Which wizard layout works better?" is a visual question â€” use the browser.

If they agree to the companion, read the detailed guide before proceeding:
`skills/brainstorming/visual-companion.md`

\title[Component Gerbes and Homological Quotients]{Component Gerbes and Homological Quotients over Teams and Presheaves}
\author{Haobo Ma}
\address[Haobo Ma]{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1, 8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981}
\email[Haobo Ma]{auric@aelf.io}

\author{Wenlin Zhang}
\address[Wenlin Zhang]{National University of Singapore, Singapore}
\email[Wenlin Zhang]{e1327962@u.nus.edu}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
On the chosen good-cover site of a wedge of $\beta$ $2$-spheres, a nonzero finite abelian group $G$ occurs as a two-component label-loss kernel with complementary nonzero image subgroups exactly when $d(G)\leq 2\beta$ and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group.  This follows from the universal coefficient theorem after fixing a finite nerve, a constant finite abelian band, and a banded groupoid lift of a presheaf.  For a component-gerbe class $\omega$, evaluation on $H_2$ gives $\operatorname{ev}_\omega:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A$; its image and cokernel determine the componentwise and aggregate quotients.  When $H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0$ and $H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, the possible image subgroups are exactly those generated by at most $\beta$ elements.  Under explicit separatedness, representability, and \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison hypotheses, matching families without global amalgamations correspond to non-neutral component gerbes.  This derived $H^2$ invariant is not the relative \v{C}ech $H^1$ obstruction of Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa; only the underlying support-presheaf comparison is functorial, and the finite-group characterization does not transfer to bare empirical models.
\end{abstract}

\keywords{teams, presheaves, sheafification, banded gerbes, homological cokernel, universal coefficient theorem}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
This document contains the supporting material relocated from the article:
the presheaf, sheafification, banded-lift, terminal-rigidity, typed-readout,
and lower-fragment packages; the cocycle-coordinate strictification results;
the finite-site constructions realizing prescribed and mixed componentwise
homological images; and the refinement-dynamics and GHZ/Mermin appendices.
The article retains the component-gerbe criterion, intrinsic homological
quotients, aggregation exact sequence, wedge-of-spheres classification, and
empirical-model comparison.  Cross-document citations are descriptive and do
not import either document's auxiliary file.
\end{abstract}

\maketitle

\section*{Dependency and source scope}

The supplement inputs exactly the source units removed from the article.
The initial section develops the set-valued and groupoid-valued local-to-global
machinery.  The next package gives the presentation-level strictification and
the finite-site existence constructions used by the article's intrinsic and
wedge-of-spheres results.  The appendices collect refinement dynamics and the
chosen split GHZ/Mermin lift.

\input{sec_mixed_component_construction}

"""Finite and symbolic checks for the claims in oracle_sprint_A9_r1.md.

These checks verify only the algebraic consequences of UCT naturality and
NWW's displayed Cechization formula.  They do not prove either open
crossed-module comparison in NWW, Problems 8.1(b) and 8.2.
"""

from fractions import Fraction
from functools import reduce
from itertools import combinations, product
from math import gcd


def _continuous_three_cocycle(a, b, c):
    """A normalized continuous inhomogeneous 3-cocycle on (R,+)."""

    return a * b * c


def _group_coboundary(w, x, y, z):
    f = _continuous_three_cocycle
    return (
        f(x, y, z)
        - f(w + x, y, z)
        + f(w, x + y, z)
        - f(w, x, y + z)
        + f(w, x, y)
    )


def _tau(g, h, k, x):
    f = _continuous_three_cocycle
    return (
        f(h, k - h, x - k)
        - f(g, k - g, x - k)
        + f(g, h - g, x - h)
    )


def _tau_rewrite(g, h, k, x):
    f = _continuous_three_cocycle
    return f(g, h - g, k - h) + f(h - g, k - h, x - k)


def check_real_cechization_identities():
    values = tuple(Fraction(i, 2) for i in range(-4, 5))
    group_cases = 0
    tau_cases = 0
    cech_cases = 0

    for w, x, y, z in product(values, repeat=4):
        assert _group_coboundary(w, x, y, z) == 0
        group_cases += 1

    for g, h, k, x in product(values, repeat=4):
        assert _tau(g, h, k, x) == _tau_rewrite(g, h, k, x)
        tau_cases += 1

    for g, h, k, ell, x in product(values, repeat=5):
        cech_delta = (
            _tau(h, k, ell, x)
            - _tau(g, k, ell, x)
            + _tau(g, h, ell, x)
            - _tau(g, h, k, x)
        )
        assert cech_delta == 0
        cech_cases += 1

    return {
        "group_cocycle_cases": group_cases,
        "tau_rewrite_cases": tau_cases,
        "cech_cocycle_cases": cech_cases,
    }


def _divisors(n):
    return tuple(d for d in range(1, n + 1) if n % d == 0)


def _cyclic_subgroup(n, generators):
    divisor = reduce(gcd, generators, n)
    return frozenset(range(0, n, divisor))


def check_finite_quotient_claims(max_modulus=36):
    factorization_cases = 0
    exact_sequence_cases = 0

    for n in range(1, max_modulus + 1):
        subgroups = tuple(_cyclic_subgroup(n, (d,)) for d in _divisors(n))

        for subgroup in subgroups:
            for quotient_modulus in _divisors(n):
                units = tuple(
                    u for u in range(quotient_modulus) if gcd(u, quotient_modulus) == 1
                ) or (0,)
                for unit in units:
                    kills_image = all((unit * x) % quotient_modulus == 0 for x in subgroup)
                    factors_through = subgroup <= frozenset(
                        x
                        for x in range(n)
                        if (unit * x) % quotient_modulus == 0
                    )
                    pushed_evaluation_is_zero = kills_image
                    assert kills_image == factors_through == pushed_evaluation_is_zero
                    factorization_cases += 1

        for first, second, third in product(subgroups, repeat=3):
            intersection = first & second & third
            subgroup_sum = _cyclic_subgroup(n, tuple(first | second | third))
            assert intersection <= subgroup_sum

            labelled_quotient_order = n // len(intersection)
            common_quotient_order = n // len(subgroup_sum)
            kernel_order = len(subgroup_sum) // len(intersection)
            assert labelled_quotient_order == kernel_order * common_quotient_order
            exact_sequence_cases += 1

    return {
        "factorization_cases": factorization_cases,
        "exact_sequence_cases": exact_sequence_cases,
    }


def _elements(moduli):
    return tuple(product(*(range(modulus) for modulus in moduli)))


def _add(left, right, moduli):
    return tuple((x + y) % modulus for x, y, modulus in zip(left, right, moduli))


def _generated_subgroup(moduli, generators):
    zero = tuple(0 for _ in moduli)
    generated = {zero}
    frontier = [zero]
    while frontier:
        current = frontier.pop()
        for generator in generators:
            candidate = _add(current, generator, moduli)
            if candidate not in generated:
                generated.add(candidate)
                frontier.append(candidate)
    return frozenset(generated)


def _all_subgroups(moduli):
    elements = _elements(moduli)
    zero = tuple(0 for _ in moduli)
    subgroups = []
    for size in range(1, len(elements) + 1):
        for subset_tuple in combinations(elements, size):
            subset = frozenset(subset_tuple)
            if zero not in subset:
                continue
            if all(_add(x, y, moduli) in subset for x in subset for y in subset):
                subgroups.append(subset)
    return tuple(subgroups)


def _minimum_generator_number(moduli, subgroup):
    elements = tuple(subgroup)
    for beta in range(len(elements) + 1):
        if any(
            _generated_subgroup(moduli, generators) == subgroup
            for generators in product(elements, repeat=beta)
        ):
            return beta
    raise AssertionError("finite subgroup has no finite generating tuple")


def check_generator_bound_classification():
    moduli_list = ((2,), (3,), (4,), (2, 2), (2, 3), (2, 2, 2), (2, 4))
    classification_cases = 0

    for moduli in moduli_list:
        elements = _elements(moduli)
        subgroups = _all_subgroups(moduli)
        minimum_generators = {
            subgroup: _minimum_generator_number(moduli, subgroup)
            for subgroup in subgroups
        }

        for beta in range(4):
            homomorphism_images = {
                _generated_subgroup(moduli, generators)
                for generators in product(elements, repeat=beta)
            }
            bounded_generator_subgroups = {
                subgroup
                for subgroup, number in minimum_generators.items()
                if number <= beta
            }
            assert homomorphism_images == bounded_generator_subgroups
            classification_cases += len(subgroups)

    return {"groups": len(moduli_list), "classification_cases": classification_cases}


def pullback_strict_inclusion_example():
    # ev: H_r(S^r v S^r) = Z^2 -> Z/2 is projection to the first factor.
    ambient_image = frozenset({0, 1})
    # Pull back along the inclusion of the second sphere: t |-> (0,t).
    pullback_image = frozenset({0})
    assert pullback_image < ambient_image
    return {
        "ambient_image": ambient_image,
        "pullback_image": pullback_image,
        "ambient_quotient_order": 2 // len(ambient_image),
        "pullback_quotient_order": 2 // len(pullback_image),
    }


def action_lift_peiffer_counterexample():
    # G = K = C_2 and K-hat = C_2 x C_2 with boundary (a,b) |-> a.
    def boundary(element):
        return element[0]

    def action(group_element, element):
        a, b = element
        return (a, (b + group_element * a) % 2)

    def multiply(left, right):
        return ((left[0] + right[0]) % 2, (left[1] + right[1]) % 2)

    def inverse(element):
        return element  # Every element of C_2 x C_2 has order at most two.

    elements = tuple(product(range(2), repeat=2))
    covers_conjugation = all(
        boundary(action(g, element)) == boundary(element)
        for g in range(2)
        for element in elements
    )
    fixes_central_kernel = all(
        action(g, (0, z)) == (0, z) for g in range(2) for z in range(2)
    )
    peiffer_identity = all(
        action(boundary(left), right)
        == multiply(multiply(left, right), inverse(left))
        for left in elements
        for right in elements
    )
    assert covers_conjugation and fixes_central_kernel and not peiffer_identity
    return {
        "covers_conjugation": covers_conjugation,
        "fixes_central_kernel": fixes_central_kernel,
        "peiffer_identity": peiffer_identity,
    }


def main():
    real = check_real_cechization_identities()
    finite = check_finite_quotient_claims()
    generators = check_generator_bound_classification()
    pullback = pullback_strict_inclusion_example()
    peiffer = action_lift_peiffer_counterexample()
    print("PASS NWW Cechization formula checks:", real)
    print("PASS finite quotient checks:", finite)
    print("PASS generator-bound checks:", generators)
    print("PASS pullback direction check:", pullback)
    print("COUNTEREXAMPLE action lift does not imply Peiffer identity:", peiffer)
    print("OPEN comparisons intentionally unverified: NWW Problems 8.1(b), 8.2(a), 8.2(b)")


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

import importlib.util
from pathlib import Path
import unittest


SCRIPT = Path(__file__).with_name("verify_A9_r1.py")


def load_verifier():
    spec = importlib.util.spec_from_file_location("verify_A9_r1", SCRIPT)
    if spec is None or spec.loader is None:
        raise RuntimeError(f"cannot load {SCRIPT}")
    module = importlib.util.module_from_spec(spec)
    spec.loader.exec_module(module)
    return module


class OracleA9R1VerificationTests(unittest.TestCase):
    def test_real_cechization_identities(self):
        verifier = load_verifier()
        result = verifier.check_real_cechization_identities()
        self.assertGreater(result["group_cocycle_cases"], 0)
        self.assertGreater(result["tau_rewrite_cases"], 0)
        self.assertGreater(result["cech_cocycle_cases"], 0)

    def test_finite_quotient_and_exact_sequence_claims(self):
        verifier = load_verifier()
        result = verifier.check_finite_quotient_claims(max_modulus=36)
        self.assertGreater(result["factorization_cases"], 0)
        self.assertGreater(result["exact_sequence_cases"], 0)

    def test_generator_bound_classification(self):
        verifier = load_verifier()
        result = verifier.check_generator_bound_classification()
        self.assertGreater(result["groups"], 0)
        self.assertGreater(result["classification_cases"], 0)

    def test_pullback_is_not_image_equality(self):
        verifier = load_verifier()
        self.assertEqual(
            verifier.pullback_strict_inclusion_example(),
            {
                "ambient_image": frozenset({0, 1}),
                "pullback_image": frozenset({0}),
                "ambient_quotient_order": 1,
                "pullback_quotient_order": 2,
            },
        )

    def test_action_lift_need_not_satisfy_peiffer_identity(self):
        verifier = load_verifier()
        result = verifier.action_lift_peiffer_counterexample()
        self.assertTrue(result["covers_conjugation"])
        self.assertTrue(result["fixes_central_kernel"])
        self.assertFalse(result["peiffer_identity"])


if __name__ == "__main__":
    unittest.main()

\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:conclusion}

The paper separates three stages of the local-to-global problem. At the level
of information states,
\suppresult{Parameter-free one-reference lower-fragment separation in the constant-free reduct}
shows that neither
existence of a matching family nor matching without amalgamation is definable
in the automorphism-invariant, constant-free, one-variable fragment considered
here. This is not a general expressivity theorem; it identifies the first
point at which the presheaf data exceed the lower language of universal team
satisfaction.

The passage from set-valued to groupoid-valued data necessarily depends on
additional lift data. By
\suppresult{Rigidity of representative-rigid lifts over separated slice sites}, a strictly
pullback-stable representative choice together with essential surjectivity on
the terminal fibre implies that the sheafification unit is surjective there.
Under $H^1$-vanishing and separatedness of the restriction to the slice site,
this is equivalent to the presheaf already satisfying the sheaf condition at
that fibre. For finite empirical models,
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface} gives a canonical split banded
stack lift, but every component gerbe is neutral. The AMB obstruction is a
relative \v{C}ech $H^1$ torsor class indexed by a local section, whereas the
obstruction here is a derived Giraud $H^2$ class indexed by a global component
section. Only the support-level comparison is functorial, and neither class
recovers the other in general.

For a fixed finite nerve representative, the homological image
$K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ and homological
cokernel $Q_\omega=A/K_\omega$ record the contribution detected on $H_2$.
Across presentations they transport along chosen arrows in the
presentation-comparison groupoid; no independence of the chosen zigzag is
asserted. For several global component sections, the label-aware and
common-character quotients are related by the component-forgetting exact
sequence. Under the fixed finite-nerve, constant-band,
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison, and $H_1=0$ hypotheses, the construction
theorems attain exactly the componentwise image subgroups permitted by
second homology.

When $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, these are precisely
the subgroups generated by at most $\beta$ elements. The chosen open-star good
covers of a wedge of $\beta$ $2$-spheres therefore give the exact
classification: the nonzero finite abelian groups occurring as two-component
label-loss kernels with complementary nonzero image subgroups are those with
$d(G)\leq2\beta$ that are not cyclic $p$-groups. This construction does not
transfer to bare empirical models, whose canonical split lift has zero
homological image subgroups.

\begin{remark}[External crossed-module comparison interface]
\label{rem:crossed-module-comparison-interface}
Let
\[
1\longrightarrow Z\longrightarrow \widehat K\longrightarrow K\longrightarrow 1
\]
be a central extension, let $K\trianglelefteq G$, put $Q=G/K$, and suppose
that $G\to Q$ admits the local sections required in locally continuous or
locally smooth group cohomology and that the conjugation action lifts so that
$\widehat K\to G$ is a crossed module.  Neeb, Wagemann, and Wockel construct
a \v{C}echization homomorphism
\[
\tau:H^3_{\mathrm{loc}}(Q,Z)\longrightarrow
\check H^2(Q,\underline Z)
\]
and ask whether the image of the crossed-module characteristic class agrees,
up to a convention-dependent sign, with the lifting obstruction of the
principal $K$-bundle $G\to Q$ \cite[Problem~8.1(b)]{NeebWagemannWockel2013}.
The present paper supplies only the downstream analysis of a lifting-gerbe
$H^2$-class after a finite-nerve, constant finite-band presentation and its
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison have been chosen.  It does not supply a chain
model for $H^3_{\mathrm{loc}}$, a representative of the crossed-module
characteristic class, or a chain homotopy comparing that representative with
the triple-overlap defect cocycle.  Consequently, neither the comparison
identity, its sign, nor its asserted naturality follows from the results
proved here.  Establishing such a comparison is an external open interface,
not a theorem or a construction begun in this paper.
\end{remark}

The hypotheses remain explicit throughout: \textup{(RC)} specifies pullbacks
of the presheaf data; \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H4)} specify the banded lift,
representability, and \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison; separatedness is invoked
where global component sections are represented by matching families; and
$H^1$-vanishing is used for terminal-fibre rigidity. The formulation here uses
pullback-based slice sites. Comparisons of finite presentations are therefore
groupoid-indexed and zigzag-dependent, rather than presentation-free choices
of canonical representatives.

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Fix an integer $\beta\geq 1$.  Our main classification theorem concerns the
good-cover intersection site obtained from barycentric open-star covers of a
finite model of the wedge $\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2$.  A nonzero finite
abelian group $G$ occurs as the kernel of the canonical map
\[
 A/\bigl(K_1\cap K_2\bigr)\longrightarrow A/\bigl(K_1+K_2\bigr)
\]
for two non-neutral component gerbes with $K_1\cap K_2=0$ and
$K_1+K_2=G$ if and only if
\[
 d(G)\leq 2\beta
 \qquad\text{and}\qquad
 G\text{ is not a cyclic }p\text{-group}.
\]
Here $d(G)$ is the minimal number of generators of $G$.  In the terminology
fixed below, this kernel is the \emph{component-forgetting kernel}; the
displayed special case is a two-component label-loss kernel with complementary
nonzero image subgroups.  The statement is an existence and classification
result for the chosen site, band, and lift data, not an invariant of an
arbitrary empirical model.

The standard input is as follows.  We use universal satisfaction on nonempty
teams of assignments, presheaves and their sheafifications, stackification of
categories fibred in groupoids, the classification of banded gerbes by
Giraud's $H^2$, the \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison on the specified Leray
covers, the universal coefficient theorem, and elementary structure theory
and duality for finite abelian groups
\cite{Giraud1971,Breen2006,Hatcher2002}.  Our contributions are the organization
of these inputs into the stated semantic framework; the comparison between
matching families and component gerbes under explicit lift hypotheses; the
presentation-comparison groupoid and the two aggregate quotients; the
component-forgetting exact sequence; the construction of prescribed
componentwise homological images; and the preceding finite-group
classification.

The relation with the Abramsky--Mansfield--Barbosa obstruction must be stated
at the outset.  Their obstruction is a relative \v{C}ech class
\[
 \gamma_e(s)\in
 \check H^1(\mathcal M,\mathcal F_{e,\overline C_0})
\]
of a presheaf, equivalently a torsor class indexed by a chosen local section
\cite{AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,Caru2017}.  Our obstruction is the
derived Giraud class
\[
 \omega_v=[\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C/a,\Band)
\]
of a gerbe banded by a sheaf, indexed by a global component section.  These
classes do not correspond.  What is functorial is only the support-level
comparison: every finite Abramsky--Brandenburger empirical model gives a
canonical split stack whose component gerbes are neutral.  In particular, a
strongly contextual model has no global component section from which our map
could produce its local-section-indexed AMB classes.  Consequently the
condition $d(G)\leq2\beta$ with $G$ not a cyclic $p$-group does not transfer to
bare empirical models; a band and a non-split banded stack lift would have to
be supplied as additional data.  This is proved precisely in
\Cref{thm:empirical-model-stack-interface,thm:unique-branch-contextuality-comparison}.

We now describe the framework.  An information state
$p=(\Gamma_p,R_p)$ is a nonempty team $R_p$ of assignments with common finite
context $\Gamma_p$.  We write $M,p\Vdash\varphi$ when every assignment in
$R_p$ satisfies $\varphi$ in the ordinary Tarskian sense.  Thus $\Vdash$ is
universal team satisfaction, not set-theoretic, Kripke, Beth, or
Kripke--Joyal forcing.  Refinement enlarges the context and may restrict the
team, which immediately gives persistence.  Point assignments enter through
the singleton-state embedding.  The later semantic frameworks add typed data
while preserving the truth of formulas in the reduct; we call these
truth-preserving reduct expansions because consequence conservativity is not asserted without an expansion
theorem for every lower model.

For an admissible parameter $r$ at $p$, the paper specifies a thin
reference-refinement site $\mathcal C_{p,s}$, its base object
$a=[r]_p$, and a presheaf of local sections
$F_{p,s}:\mathcal C_{p,s}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}$.  A matching family
without an element of $F_{p,s}(a)$ is the set-valued failure studied here.
The forward implication from a matching family to a section of the
sheafification is formal; the converse holds when
$F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_{p,s}/a}$ is separated
(\suppresult{Existence of a matching family gives a sheafified section} and
\suppresult{Sheafification characterizes existence of a matching family on separated slice sites}).
The predicates expressing a matching family, a global section, and matching
without amalgamation are not definable in the automorphism-invariant,
constant-free, one-variable fragment of the lower reduct
(\suppresult{Parameter-free one-reference lower-fragment separation in the constant-free reduct}).

Gerbe-valued obstructions require extra, noncanonical structure.  A
gluing-sensitive banded lift datum consists of a banded groupoid lift of $F$,
its stackification together with an identification of its component sheaf
with $aF$, essential surjectivity on the terminal fibre, and a
\v{C}ech-to-derived comparison hypothesis.  A section
$v\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)\cong(aF)(a)$ determines a full component gerbe
$\mathfrak G_v$ and hence a Giraud class $\omega_v$.  Under the stated
separatedness, representability, and comparison hypotheses, matching without
amalgamation is equivalent to the existence of global component sections with
all corresponding component gerbes non-neutral
(\Cref{thm:component-gerbe-decomposition,thm:gerbe-null-semantics}).  A
strictly pullback-stable representative choice imposes a useful rigidity
condition: together with terminal-fibre essential surjectivity it forces the
sheafification unit to be onto at the terminal object
(\suppresult{Rigidity of representative-rigid lifts over separated slice sites}).

Fix next a global component section, a finite nerve representative, and a
constant finite abelian band $A$.  The universal coefficient map evaluates
the class on second homology,
\[
 \operatorname{ev}_{\omega}:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\longrightarrow A.
\]
We write $K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ for its
homological image and $Q_\omega=\operatorname{coker}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)
=A/K_\omega$ for its homological cokernel.  These are invariants of an object
of the presentation-comparison groupoid and transport functorially along the
chosen comparison zigzags; no path independence is claimed.  A class with
zero $H_2$ evaluation is precisely a class in the $\operatorname{Ext}^1$
kernel of the universal coefficient sequence.  This separates the
homology-evaluation quotient from the more presentation-dependent quotient by
the subgroup generated by the coordinates of a chosen cocycle.

For finitely many component sections $v$, put $K_v=\operatorname{Im}
(\operatorname{ev}_{\omega_v})$.  Retaining the component label gives the
label-aware quotient $A/(\bigcap_vK_v)$; retaining only characters that work
for every component gives the common-character quotient
$A/(\sum_vK_v)$.  Their canonical comparison has kernel
\[
 \frac{\sum_vK_v}{\bigcap_vK_v}.
\]
The exact sequence and its finite-register interpretation are formal.  The
existence results are not: on the finite intersection site associated to a
connected good-cover nerve with $H_1=0$, we construct typed expansions whose
componentwise evaluation maps have prescribed images, including mixtures of
neutral and non-neutral component gerbes.  If $H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong
\mathbb Z^\beta$, the attainable image subgroups are exactly those generated
by at most $\beta$ elements.  The main classification follows by asking when
$G$ decomposes as $K_1\oplus K_2$ with both summands nonzero and each generated
by at most $\beta$ elements.

The paper is organized as follows.  Sections~\ref{sec:preliminaries} and
\ref{sec:information-states} define the semantic frameworks and universal
team satisfaction.  The article then gives the component-gerbe and
Giraud-class results, the finite-presentation and homological quotients, their
aggregation over component sections, the wedge-of-spheres classification, and
the precise empirical-model comparison.  The separately compiled
\suppresult{Presheaves, sheafification, and matching without amalgamation}
contains the full presheaf/sheafification, banded-lift, terminal-rigidity,
typed-readout, null-trichotomy, and lower-fragment separation packages.
It also contains the sections titled ``Cocycle-coordinate and component-image
constructions'' and ``Refinement dynamics,'' which prove the
cocycle-coordinate strictification results, the prescribed and mixed
component-image construction theorems, the refinement-dynamics consequences,
and the chosen GHZ/Mermin lift.

\subsection*{Glossary of retained terminology}

The following short names are used only after their standard-language content
has been fixed.

\begin{description}[style=nextline,leftmargin=2em,labelindent=0pt]
\item[Information state $p=(\Gamma,R)$.]
A nonempty team $R$ of assignments with common finite context $\Gamma$.
\item[Universal team satisfaction.]
$M,p\Vdash\varphi$ means that every assignment in the team of $p$ satisfies
$\varphi$ in ordinary Tarskian semantics.
\item[Singleton-state embedding.]
The map from an assignment to its singleton team, preserving ordinary
satisfaction.
\item[Truth-preserving reduct expansion.]
An expansion whose higher models preserve lower-language satisfaction after
the specified model reduct and state projection; consequence conservativity
is not part of the definition.
\item[Typed expansion class.]
The class of all supplied higher-layer data of the stipulated types over a
fixed lower model.
\item[Admissible parameter $r$.]
An element satisfying the primitive predicate $\Adm_s$; the predicate records
exactly that the site and presheaf data stipulated in the relevant typed model
are attached to $r$.
\item[{Base object $a=[r]_p$.}]
The object of the reference-refinement site associated to the pair $(p,r)$;
the brackets do not denote an equivalence class.
\item[Reference-refinement site $\mathcal C_{p,s}$.]
The thin category of base objects ordered by refinement, equipped with the
specified pretopology and pullbacks.
\item[Presheaf of local sections $F_{p,s}$.]
The functor $\mathcal C_{p,s}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}$.
\item[Matching without amalgamation at $a$.]
A matching family exists on a cover of $a$, but $F_{p,s}(a)$ is empty.
\item[Gluing-sensitive banded lift datum.]
A banded groupoid lift, its stackification and component-sheaf identification,
terminal-fibre essential surjectivity, and a \v{C}ech-to-derived comparison
hypothesis.
\item[Pullback-stable representative choice.]
A choice of one representative of each presheaf section that is preserved
strictly by every pullback; ``representative-rigid'' abbreviates this condition.
\item[Global component section $v$.]
An element of $\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)$, identified with a section of
$(aF)(a)$.
\item[Component gerbe $\mathfrak G_v$ and component obstruction $\omega_v$.]
The full banded substack over $v$ and its Giraud class
$[\mathfrak G_v]\in H^2(\mathcal C/a,\Band)$.
\item[Presentation-comparison groupoid.]
The groupoid freely generated by the specified refinements, cocycle
replacements, band retrivializations, and their formal inverses; distinct
zigzags need not give the same comparison.
\item[Coordinate-image subgroup and cocycle-coordinate quotient.]
For a chosen cocycle $\alpha$, the subgroup generated by its coordinate values
and the corresponding quotient $A/H_\alpha$.
\item[Homological image $K_\omega$ and homological cokernel $Q_\omega$.]
$\operatorname{Im}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)$ and
$\operatorname{coker}(\operatorname{ev}_\omega)=A/K_\omega$.
\item[Label-aware quotient.]
$A/(\bigcap_vK_v)$, equivalently the image of $A$ in the product of the
componentwise homological cokernels.
\item[Common-character quotient.]
$A/(\sum_vK_v)$, detected by the characters that annihilate every $K_v$.
\item[Component-forgetting kernel.]
The kernel of the canonical map from the label-aware quotient to the
common-character quotient, namely $(\sum_vK_v)/(\bigcap_vK_v)$.
\item[Fibre-separating auxiliary register.]
A finite set used in an injective lift of a quotient map; its minimum
cardinality is the largest quotient-fibre cardinality.
\item[Finite intersection site associated to a cover nerve.]
The finite thin pullback site generated by the formal intersections indexed by
the nerve, with an initial object representing empty intersections.
\item[Typed readout.]
The partial output attached to an admissible parameter: inadmissible if no
typed data are attached, uniquely determined if the sheafified global section
is unique, and non-unique-or-absent otherwise.
\item[Indexed refinement system.]
A family of state-refinement relations indexed by information coordinates,
each monotone for the state preorder and the specified time order.
\item[Information-determining axis set.]
A finite set of refinement coordinates whose information values determine any
decided truth value among the states reachable along those coordinates.
\item[Pairwise commuting refinements and common refinement.]
Refinements for which every indicated finite family has a state refining all
its members.
\item[Readout-preserving rewrite.]
A rewrite between partial outputs that preserves both definedness and value.
\item[Banded groupoid lift of a support presheaf.]
A category fibred in groupoids whose presheaf of isomorphism classes is the
given empirical-model support presheaf and whose automorphism sheaves are the
specified band.
\end{description}


 succeeded in 207ms:
\section{Semantic frameworks and reduct expansions}\label{sec:preliminaries}

We fix the semantic framework used throughout. In particular, we distinguish truth-preserving reduct expansion from the weaker singleton embedding that links the pointwise layer to universal team satisfaction.

\subsection*{Standing notation}
States are denoted by $p,q,u$, and admissible parameters by $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ with associated base object $a=[r]_p$. The reference-refinement site at a state and sort is $\mathcal C_{p,s}$, the presheaf of local sections is $F_{p,s}$, the set of global component sections is $\Vis_{p,s}(r)$, and component obstruction classes are denoted by $\omega$ or $\omega_v$.

\begin{definition}[Semantic framework]\label{def:logic-layer}
A \emph{semantic framework} is a tuple
\[
\mathbb{L}=(\Sigma,\mathrm{Form},\Mod,\St,\Vdash,\models),
\]
where $\Sigma$ is a signature, $\mathrm{Form}$ is the class of well-formed $\Sigma$-formulas, $\Mod$ is a class of admissible models, $\St$ assigns to every $M\in\Mod$ a nonempty set $\St(M)$ of states, $\Vdash$ is a satisfaction relation on model-state pairs, and $\models$ is the induced consequence relation defined by
\[
\Gamma\models\varphi
\quad:\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\forall M\in\Mod\;\forall p\in\St(M)\,
\bigl(M,p\Vdash\Gamma \Rightarrow M,p\Vdash\varphi\bigr).
\]
\end{definition}

Satisfaction is the primitive notion; consequence is derived from it.

\begin{definition}[Truth-preserving reduct expansion]\label{def:conservative-extension}
Let
\[
\mathbb{L}_m=(\Sigma_m,\mathrm{Form}_m,\Mod_m,\St_m,\Vdash_m,\models_m)
\quad\text{and}\quad
\mathbb{L}_n=(\Sigma_n,\mathrm{Form}_n,\Mod_n,\St_n,\Vdash_n,\models_n)
\]
with $m<n$. We say that $\mathbb{L}_n$ is a \emph{truth-preserving reduct expansion} of $\mathbb{L}_m$, written $\mathbb{L}_m\preceq\mathbb{L}_n$, if there exist
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item a language embedding $e_{m,n}:\Sigma_m\hookrightarrow\Sigma_n$,
\item a forgetful functor $U_{n,m}:\Mod_n\to\Mod_m$,
\item for every $M\in\Mod_n$, a state projection
\[
\pi^M_{n,m}:\St_n(M)\to \St_m\bigl(U_{n,m}(M)\bigr),
\]
\end{enumerate}
such that for every $\varphi\in \mathrm{Form}_m$, every $M\in\Mod_n$, and every $p\in\St_n(M)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conservative-extension}
M,p\Vdash_n \varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{n,m}(M),\pi^M_{n,m}(p)\Vdash_m \varphi.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Thus a higher layer may add coordinates or geometric data, but lower-layer formulas are interpreted unchanged after projection.

\begin{definition}[Singleton-state embedding]\label{def:singleton-semantic-embedding}
Let $\mathbb{L}_0=(\Sigma_0,\mathrm{Form}_0,\Mod_0,\St_0,\Vdash_0,\models_0)$ and $\mathbb{L}_1=(\Sigma_1,\mathrm{Form}_1,\Mod_1,\St_1,\Vdash_1,\models_1)$. We say that $\mathbb{L}_0$ \emph{embeds into $\mathbb{L}_1$ by a singleton-state embedding}, written $\mathbb{L}_0\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}_1$, if there exist
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item a language embedding $e_{0,1}:\Sigma_0\hookrightarrow\Sigma_1$,
\item a forgetful functor $U_{1,0}:\Mod_1\to\Mod_0$,
\item for every $M\in\Mod_1$ and every pointwise state $\rho\in\St_0(U_{1,0}(M))$, a canonical singleton state
\[
\iota_{0,1}^M(\rho)\in\St_1(M)
\]
\end{enumerate}
such that for every $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:singleton-semantic-embedding}
M,\iota_{0,1}^M(\rho)\Vdash_1\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{1,0}(M),\rho\Vdash_0\varphi.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

A singleton-state embedding is weaker than a truth-preserving reduct expansion: it compares only singleton states, and no projection from arbitrary information states to pointwise states is required.

\begin{proposition}[Composition of truth-preserving reduct expansions]\label{prop:conservative-composition}
If $\mathbb{L}_\ell\preceq\mathbb{L}_m$ and $\mathbb{L}_m\preceq\mathbb{L}_n$, then $\mathbb{L}_\ell\preceq\mathbb{L}_n$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Compose the language embeddings and forgetful functors:
\[
e_{\ell,n}:=e_{m,n}\circ e_{\ell,m},
\qquad
U_{n,\ell}:=U_{m,\ell}\circ U_{n,m}.
\]
For every $M\in\Mod_n$, define
\[
\pi^M_{n,\ell}:=
\pi^{\,U_{n,m}(M)}_{m,\ell}\circ \pi^M_{n,m}.
\]
If $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_\ell$ and $p\in\St_n(M)$, then
\[
M,p\Vdash_n\varphi
\Longleftrightarrow
U_{n,m}(M),\pi^M_{n,m}(p)\Vdash_m\varphi
\Longleftrightarrow
U_{n,\ell}(M),\pi^M_{n,\ell}(p)\Vdash_\ell\varphi,
\]
by two applications of \Cref{def:conservative-extension}. This is exactly the required truth-preservation condition.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite-chain invariance]\label{cor:whole-chain-invariance}
If
\[
\mathbb{L}_0\preceq\mathbb{L}_1\preceq\cdots\preceq\mathbb{L}_n,
\]
then every formula belonging to any lower layer keeps the same satisfaction behavior when interpreted in any later layer and projected back down.
\leanverified{paper_conservative_extension_whole_chain_invariance_apal}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply \Cref{prop:conservative-composition} repeatedly along the chain.
\end{proof}

The semantic frameworks considered later fit into the chain
\[
\mathbb{L}_0
\xhookrightarrow{\iota}
\mathbb{L}_1
\preceq
\mathbb{L}_2
\preceq
\mathbb{L}_3
\preceq
\mathbb{L}_4,
\]
where the hooked arrow $\xhookrightarrow{\iota}$ denotes a singleton-state embedding (\Cref{def:singleton-semantic-embedding}) and each $\preceq$ denotes a truth-preserving reduct expansion in the sense of \Cref{def:conservative-extension}. Here $\mathbb{L}_0$ is the underlying pointwise semantics, $\mathbb{L}_1$ uses universal team satisfaction and has parameter sorts, $\mathbb{L}_2$ adds local-section predicates and site structure, $\mathbb{L}_3$ adds matching-without-amalgamation and obstruction data, and $\mathbb{L}_4$ adds refinement dynamics. By \Cref{cor:whole-chain-invariance}, the passage from $\mathbb{L}_1$ upward is cumulative. The first step is weaker: pointwise states embed faithfully as singleton information states, but arbitrary nonsingleton teams need not project back to single assignments.

The model classes of the higher layers are used below as generated
expansion classes.
Precisely, an expansion from $\mathbb L_m$ to $\mathbb L_n$ is admissible when
the newly adjoined symbols of $\Sigma_n\setminus\Sigma_m$ are interpreted by
the kind of data named for that layer, the reduct under $U_{n,m}$ is the given
lower model, and the lower atomic satisfaction clauses are read through that
reduct.  No existence theorem for gluing-sensitive data is being smuggled into
this convention: the convention says only that, once such data are explicitly
constructed or assumed and satisfy the stated typing axioms, the resulting
structure is a member of the relevant higher model class.

Accordingly, the construction theorems in later sections are internal
construction results under this typed expansion convention. They first
construct typed finite sites, presheaves, prestacks, stackifications, and
cohomology classes satisfying the displayed hypotheses, and then invoke the
expansion convention to regard those data as an \(\mathbb L_2\)- or
\(\mathbb L_3\)-model. They should not be read as closure theorems for an
independently specified semantic model class.

\begin{lemma}[Flat reduct induction for a displayed layer extension]\label{lem:layer-reduct-induction}
Let $1\le m<n\le 4$. Suppose that $\Sigma_n$ is obtained from $\Sigma_m$ by adjoining a disjoint block of new symbols, that $\Mod_n$ consists of expansions of $\Sigma_m$-reducts through the forgetful functor
\[
U_{n,m}:\Mod_n\to\Mod_m,
\]
and that the state projection
\[
\pi^M_{n,m}:\St_n(M)\to\St_m(U_{n,m}(M))
\]
forgets only the new layer coordinates. Assume also that every atomic satisfaction clause whose atom belongs to $\Sigma_m$ is interpreted through the reduct:
\[
M,p\Vdash_n \alpha
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{n,m}(M),\pi^M_{n,m}(p)\Vdash_m \alpha
\]
for every $\Sigma_m$-atomic formula $\alpha$. Then the same equivalence holds for every formula $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_m$:
\[
M,p\Vdash_n \varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{n,m}(M),\pi^M_{n,m}(p)\Vdash_m \varphi.
\]
Consequently $\mathbb L_m\preceq\mathbb L_n$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For every retained formula \(\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_m\), satisfaction is the
universal team satisfaction of \Cref{def:state-forcing}: it means
ordinary pointwise satisfaction of \(\varphi\) by every assignment in the
state. The reduct functor \(U_{n,m}\) leaves all retained symbols, retained
sorts, and retained assignments unchanged, while the state projection
\(\pi^M_{n,m}\) forgets only the newly adjoined layer coordinates. Hence, for
every retained assignment \(\rho\) occurring in \(p\),
\[
M,\rho\models \varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{n,m}(M),\rho|_{\Sigma_m}\models \varphi .
\]
This is the ordinary first-order reduct induction on the formula
\(\varphi\): atomic formulas are the displayed hypothesis, Boolean
connectives are immediate, and quantifiers range over the same retained
sort-carriers on both sides. Taking the universal quantifier over the same
projected team of assignments in the state gives
\[
M,p\Vdash_n\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{n,m}(M),\pi^M_{n,m}(p)\Vdash_m\varphi .
\]
Persistence and decidedness are not additional recursive satisfaction clauses in
this paper; persistence is a theorem about the flat satisfaction relation, and
decidedness is the abbreviation from \Cref{def:decidedness}. The displayed
equivalence is exactly \Cref{eq:conservative-extension}, so
\(\mathbb L_m\preceq\mathbb L_n\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Typed-expansion model-class interface for the layer chain]\label{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}
Let $i\in\{1,2,3\}$ and let $M_i\in\Mod_i$. Suppose a block of new layer data
$D_{i+1}$ is supplied as follows:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item for $i=1$, $D_2$ may be any finite pullback-based reference-refinement site
attached to the given admissible parameters, with covering families, refinement
relations, pullbacks, and any presheaves of local sections satisfying the
typing clauses of
\suppresult{Base objects},
\suppresult{Reference-refinement site}, and
\suppresult{Presheaf of local sections};
\item for $i=2$, $D_3$ consists of matching-without-amalgamation predicates,
global component sections, banded groupoid lifts, stackifications or banded
stack lifts, component-gerbe data, and obstruction classes
satisfying the hypotheses explicitly stated where those data are used;
\item for $i=3$, $D_4$ consists of refinement-dynamic axes, update relations,
information-determining axis data, and rewrite operations satisfying the typing clauses of
\suppresult{Indexed refinement system} and
\suppresult{Readout-preserving rewrite}.
\end{enumerate}
Assume in each case that the interpretations of all symbols already belonging
to $\Sigma_i$ are exactly those of $M_i$, and that every $\Sigma_i$-atomic
satisfaction clause is evaluated through the reduct. Then the expansion
\[
M_{i+1}:=M_i[D_{i+1}]
\]
is an admissible model in $\Mod_{i+1}$, satisfies
\[
U_{i+1,i}(M_{i+1})=M_i,
\]
and, with the state projection forgetting only the newly added layer
coordinates, preserves satisfaction of every $\mathbb L_i$-formula:
\[
M_{i+1},p\Vdash_{i+1}\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
M_i,\pi^{M_{i+1}}_{i+1,i}(p)\Vdash_i\varphi
\qquad(\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_i).
\]
Thus arbitrary finite pullback subsites, arbitrary presheaves of local sections on
those subsites, and arbitrary supplied banded prestack or banded stack-lift data
are admissible only in the typed expansion class defined by this proposition,
once they satisfy the displayed typing and theorem-level hypotheses. This is a
model-class convention for the expanded layers, not a semantic existence or
closure theorem for an independently restricted class of models. The construction
theorems below therefore have two parts: an algebraic construction of typed
site, presheaf, prestack, stackification, and cohomology data, followed by the
typed-expansion step which declares those well-typed data to be an
\(\mathbb L_{i+1}\)-model without changing lower-layer satisfaction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the expansion convention just fixed, $\Mod_{i+1}$ is exactly the class of
$\Sigma_{i+1}$-expansions of admissible $\Sigma_i$-models by the corresponding
well-typed layer data.  The hypotheses on $D_{i+1}$ therefore put
$M_i[D_{i+1}]$ in $\Mod_{i+1}$ and make its reduct under $U_{i+1,i}$ equal to
$M_i$.  The state projection is the projection specified for the adjacent
layer boundary and forgets only the new layer coordinates.  Since lower atomic
satisfaction clauses are assumed to be interpreted through the reduct, all
hypotheses of \Cref{lem:layer-reduct-induction} hold for the adjacent pair
$\mathbb L_i\preceq\mathbb L_{i+1}$.  Applying that lemma gives the displayed
satisfaction equivalence for every $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_i$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Concrete instantiation of the finite chain]\label{prop:chain-instantiation}
The chain
\[
\mathbb L_0\xhookrightarrow{\iota} \mathbb L_1\preceq \mathbb L_2\preceq \mathbb L_3\preceq \mathbb L_4
\]
is instantiated as follows.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=3em]
\item \textbf{$\mathbb L_0\hookrightarrow\mathbb L_1$ (pointwise states to information states: singleton-state embedding).}
The language embedding
\[
e_{0,1}:\Sigma_0\hookrightarrow \Sigma_1
\]
is the inclusion of the underlying many-sorted symbols. In addition, $\Sigma_1$ contains the parameter sorts $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ (one for each designated sort $s\in S_{\mathrm{ref}}$) together with their equality; no geometric predicates or site structure is imposed at this step. The forgetful functor
\[
U_{1,0}:\Mod_1\to\Mod_0
\]
forgets the information-state presentation and the reference carrier sets, remembering only the underlying many-sorted structure of $\Sigma_0$. A pointwise state $\rho$ in $\mathbb L_0$ is represented inside $\mathbb L_1$ by the singleton information state
\[
\iota_{0,1}^M(\rho):=(\Gamma,\{\rho\}).
\]
By \Cref{prop:singleton-conservativity},
\[
M,\iota_{0,1}^M(\rho)\Vdash_1\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{1,0}(M),\rho\Vdash_0\varphi
\]
for every $\mathbb L_0$-formula $\varphi$. This is a singleton-state embedding in the sense of \Cref{def:singleton-semantic-embedding}: it preserves satisfaction of $\mathbb L_0$-formulas on the canonical singleton image but does not provide a state projection from arbitrary $\mathbb L_1$-states back to $\mathbb L_0$-states.

\item \textbf{$\mathbb L_1\leadsto\mathbb L_2$ (information states to presheaves of local sections).}
The embedding
\[
e_{1,2}:\Sigma_1\hookrightarrow \Sigma_2
\]
adjoins the admissibility predicates, the local-section predicates $\LocSec_s$, $\CompSec_s$, $\Sec_s$, the site-structure symbols (covers, refinement preorder, pullbacks), and the associated geometric notation. The parameter sorts $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ and their equality are already present in $\Sigma_1$; the step from $\mathbb L_1$ to $\mathbb L_2$ equips admissible parameters with the geometric structure needed to state local-to-global questions. The forgetful functor
\[
U_{2,1}:\Mod_2\to\Mod_1
\]
forgets the reference-refinement sites, covers, and presheaves of local sections but retains the parameter sorts and their carrier sets. The state projection
\[
\pi^M_{2,1}:\St_2(M)\to \St_1(U_{2,1}(M))
\]
is the identity on the underlying information state $(\Gamma_p,R_p)$. Since the satisfaction clauses for formulas already in $\mathrm{Form}_1$ never inspect the added presheaf data, one has
\[
M,p\Vdash_2\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{2,1}(M),\pi^M_{2,1}(p)\Vdash_1\varphi
\]
for every $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_1$.

\item \textbf{$\mathbb L_2\leadsto\mathbb L_3$ (presheaves to obstruction data).}
The embedding
\[
e_{2,3}:\Sigma_2\hookrightarrow \Sigma_3
\]
adjoins the predicates and symbols used to talk about structural absence, global component sections, component gerbes, and obstruction classes. The forgetful functor
\[
U_{3,2}:\Mod_3\to\Mod_2
\]
drops the banded groupoid lift, band, component-gerbe, and obstruction data. The state projection
\[
\pi^M_{3,2}:\St_3(M)\to \St_2(U_{3,2}(M))
\]
again leaves the underlying state unchanged. Formulas from $\mathrm{Form}_2$ are evaluated exactly as before, so
\[
M,p\Vdash_3\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{3,2}(M),\pi^M_{3,2}(p)\Vdash_2\varphi
\]
for every $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_2$.

\item \textbf{$\mathbb L_3\leadsto\mathbb L_4$ (obstruction data to refinement dynamics).}
The embedding
\[
e_{3,4}:\Sigma_3\hookrightarrow \Sigma_4
\]
adjoins the update relations, axis indices, support notions, and rewrite language of the refinement layer. The forgetful functor
\[
U_{4,3}:\Mod_4\to\Mod_3
\]
forgets the dynamic axes, update structure, and rewrite operations. The state projection
\[
\pi^M_{4,3}:\St_4(M)\to \St_3(U_{4,3}(M))
\]
forgets only the new dynamic bookkeeping. Because the satisfaction clauses for $\mathrm{Form}_3$ do not depend on that added bookkeeping, one has
\[
M,p\Vdash_4\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
U_{4,3}(M),\pi^M_{4,3}(p)\Vdash_3\varphi
\]
for every $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_3$.
\end{enumerate}
The base boundary $\mathbb L_0\hookrightarrow\mathbb L_1$ is a singleton-state embedding: pointwise states embed faithfully into information states, preserving satisfaction of all $\mathbb L_0$-formulas on that canonical image. The three higher boundaries $\mathbb L_1\preceq\mathbb L_2\preceq\mathbb L_3\preceq\mathbb L_4$ are truth-preserving reduct expansions in the sense of \Cref{def:conservative-extension}, each with an explicit forgetful functor and state projection.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For the first boundary, the data displayed in \textup{(i)} give the language embedding
\[
e_{0,1}:\Sigma_0\hookrightarrow\Sigma_1,
\]
the forgetful functor $U_{1,0}$, and for each pointwise state $\rho$ the canonical singleton information state
\[
\iota_{0,1}^M(\rho)=(\Gamma,\{\rho\}).
\]
\Cref{prop:singleton-conservativity} gives, for every $\mathbb L_0$-formula $\varphi$,
\[
M,\iota_{0,1}^M(\rho)\Vdash_1\varphi
\Longleftrightarrow
U_{1,0}(M),\rho\Vdash_0\varphi.
\]
These are exactly the three pieces of structure and the preservation condition required in \Cref{def:singleton-semantic-embedding}. Notice that the verification uses only singleton information states; it does not assert a projection from arbitrary $\mathbb L_1$-states to pointwise states.

For $\mathbb L_1\preceq\mathbb L_2$, the displayed clauses fix the object language of the expansion: $\Sigma_2$ is the disjoint expansion of $\Sigma_1$ by the local-section and site symbols listed in \textup{(ii)}, $\Mod_2$ is the class of $\Sigma_2$-expansions whose $\Sigma_1$-reduct is obtained by $U_{2,1}$, and $\pi^M_{2,1}$ is the identity on the underlying information state. Equivalently, this is the presheaf instance of the typed-expansion interface of \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}. Atomic $\Sigma_1$-formulas use only symbols retained by $U_{2,1}$. Thus \Cref{lem:layer-reduct-induction} gives, for every $\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_1$,
\[
M,p\Vdash_2\varphi
\Longleftrightarrow
U_{2,1}(M),\pi^M_{2,1}(p)\Vdash_1\varphi,
\]
which is \Cref{def:conservative-extension}.

The same lemma applies to $\mathbb L_2\preceq\mathbb L_3$. Here $\Sigma_3$ is the disjoint expansion of $\Sigma_2$ by the banded-groupoid-lift, band, component-gerbe, matching-without-amalgamation, and obstruction-class symbols listed in \textup{(iii)}. The admissible models are precisely the corresponding well-typed expansions singled out in \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}. The functor $U_{3,2}$ forgets precisely those symbols, while $\pi^M_{3,2}$ leaves the underlying state unchanged. Atomic $\Sigma_2$-formulas contain no symbols for the forgotten gerbe or obstruction data, so \Cref{lem:layer-reduct-induction} gives
\[
M,p\Vdash_3\varphi
\Longleftrightarrow
U_{3,2}(M),\pi^M_{3,2}(p)\Vdash_2\varphi
\qquad(\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_2).
\]
Thus $\mathbb L_2\preceq\mathbb L_3$.

Finally, $\Sigma_4$ is obtained from $\Sigma_3$ by adjoining only the refinement-dynamic axes, update structure, support notions, and rewrite operations listed in \textup{(iv)}. This is the refinement-dynamic instance of \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}. The functor $U_{4,3}$ forgets exactly that dynamic structure, and $\pi^M_{4,3}$ forgets only the corresponding state bookkeeping. Atomic $\Sigma_3$-formulas are interpreted through the reduct, so \Cref{lem:layer-reduct-induction} yields
\[
M,p\Vdash_4\varphi
\Longleftrightarrow
U_{4,3}(M),\pi^M_{4,3}(p)\Vdash_3\varphi
\qquad(\varphi\in\mathrm{Form}_3).
\]
Consequently the three upper steps are truth-preserving reduct expansions, and the displayed finite chain has exactly the asserted form.
\end{proof}

Hence the truth-preserving-reduct part of the chain begins at $\mathbb L_1$.


 succeeded in 200ms:
\section{Information states and universal team satisfaction}\label{sec:information-states}

We now recall the universal team semantics on information states.

\subsection{States, refinement, and satisfaction}

Fix a many-sorted first-order signature
\[
\Sigma=(S,F,R).
\]
For a model $M$, write $D_s^M$ for the interpretation of a sort $s\in S$. A \emph{context} is a finite typed list
\[
\Gamma=(x_1:s_1,\dots,x_n:s_n),
\]
and its team of assignments in $M$ is
\[
\Val_M(\Gamma):=\{\rho\mid \rho(x_i)\in D_{s_i}^M\text{ for }1\le i\le n\}.
\]
If $\Gamma\preceq\Delta$, every $\sigma\in\Val_M(\Delta)$ restricts to an element $\sigma|_{\Gamma}\in\Val_M(\Gamma)$.

\begin{definition}[Information state]\label{def:information-state}
An \emph{information state} in $M$ is a pair
\[
p=(\Gamma_p,R_p)
\]
such that $\Gamma_p$ is a context and $\varnothing\neq R_p\subseteq \Val_M(\Gamma_p)$. The set of information states in $M$ is denoted by $\St(M)$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Refinement]\label{def:refinement}
For states $p=(\Gamma_p,R_p)$ and $q=(\Gamma_q,R_q)$ in the same model, write $q\le p$ if
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
\item $\Gamma_p\preceq\Gamma_q$,
\item every $\sigma\in R_q$ satisfies $\sigma|_{\Gamma_p}\in R_p$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

Refinement enlarges the context and may shrink the set of assignments.

\begin{definition}[Universal team satisfaction]\label{def:state-forcing}
Let $p=(\Gamma_p,R_p)\in\St(M)$ and let $\varphi$ be a formula well formed in context $\Gamma_p$. We define
\[
M,p\Vdash\varphi
\quad:\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\forall \rho\in R_p\;(M,\rho\models\varphi).
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Distinction from forcing semantics]\label{rem:flat-forcing-convention}
The relation $\Vdash$ is the universal team satisfaction relation of
\Cref{def:state-forcing}. It is not Kripke--Joyal or set-theoretic forcing:
there are no recursive forcing clauses for logical
connectives or quantifiers, and persistence below is a theorem about
universal satisfaction under refinement rather than a defining clause.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}[State theory]\label{def:state-theory}
For $p\in\St(M)$, define
\[
\Th_M(p):=\{\varphi\mid M,p\Vdash\varphi\}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Decidedness]\label{def:decidedness}
A formula $\varphi$ is \emph{decided} at $p$, written $M,p\Downarrow\varphi$, if
\[
M,p\Vdash\varphi
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
M,p\Vdash\neg\varphi.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Persistence of universal team satisfaction]\label{prop:forcing-persistence}
If $q\le p$ and $M,p\Vdash\varphi$, then $M,q\Vdash\varphi^\uparrow$, where $\varphi^\uparrow$ is the same formula viewed in the larger context $\Gamma_q$.
\leanverified{paper_information_state_forcing_persistence_seeds}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $\sigma\in R_q$. By \Cref{def:refinement}, its restriction $\sigma|_{\Gamma_p}$ lies in $R_p$. Since $M,p\Vdash\varphi$, we have $M,\sigma|_{\Gamma_p}\models\varphi$. The formula $\varphi$ mentions only variables from $\Gamma_p$, so the same assignment viewed in the larger context yields $M,\sigma\models\varphi^\uparrow$. As $\sigma$ was arbitrary, $M,q\Vdash\varphi^\uparrow$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Monotone growth of state theories]\label{cor:state-theory-monotonicity}
If $q\le p$, then $\Th_M(p)\subseteq \Th_M(q)$.
\leanverified{paper_information_state_theory_monotonicity_seeds}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Immediate from \Cref{prop:forcing-persistence}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Agreement on singleton teams]\label{prop:singleton-conservativity}
Let $\rho\in\Val_M(\Gamma)$ and put $p_\rho=(\Gamma,\{\rho\})$. Then for every $\Gamma$-formula $\varphi$,
\[
M,p_\rho\Vdash\varphi
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
M,\rho\models\varphi.
\]
\leanverified{paper_information_state_singleton_conservativity_apal}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $R_{p_\rho}=\{\rho\}$, the satisfaction clause in \Cref{def:state-forcing} reduces immediately to ordinary satisfaction at $\rho$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Lifted consequence}

\begin{definition}[State semantic consequence]\label{def:state-semantic-consequence}
For formulas $\Phi\cup\{\varphi\}$, define
\[
\Phi\models_{\mathrm{S}}\varphi
\]
to mean that for every model $M$ and every state $p\in\St(M)$,
\[
M,p\Vdash\Phi
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
M,p\Vdash\varphi.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Lifted soundness of pointwise reasoning]\label{prop:lifted-pointwise-soundness}
Let $\vdash_*$ be any derivability relation on formulas that is sound for ordinary many-sorted satisfaction. Then
\[
\Gamma\vdash_*\varphi
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\Gamma\models_{\mathrm{S}}\varphi.
\]
\leanverified{paper_information_state_lifted_pointwise_soundness_seeds}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Assume $\Gamma\vdash_*\varphi$, and let $M$ and $p=(\Gamma_p,R_p)$ be such that $M,p\Vdash\Gamma$. By definition, every assignment $\rho\in R_p$ satisfies every formula in $\Gamma$ pointwise. Since $\vdash_*$ is sound for ordinary satisfaction, each such $\rho$ also satisfies $\varphi$. Therefore $M,p\Vdash\varphi$. As $M$ and $p$ were arbitrary, $\Gamma\models_{\mathrm{S}}\varphi$.
\end{proof}
